27. The proposa l is expected to create 8,000 jobs and support livelihoods development.  
28. The proposal recognizes the important roles that women play , and has put strong 
emphasis on women’s role s and responsibilit ies. The proposal aims to remove the barriers that 
women face in decision -making, training and resource management and support women’s 
growing role in production.  
3.4 Needs of the recipient                                                                    Scale: High  
29. Pakistan is highly exposed to flooding and drought . In 2022 floods devastated the 
country ; over 1,300 lives  were  lost and over 33 million people displaced . 
30. Pakistan is facing financial constraints for climate change adaptation.   
31. The m ajority of the targeted communities are living below the poverty line in the Indus 
floodplains , which  are highly susceptible to floodi ng. 
3.5 Country ownership                                                                    Scale: High  
32. The p roject is supported at the highest levels of g overnment and is aligned with the 
National Climate Change Policy and its implementation framework. It  supports both of the 
objectives of Pakistan’s Green Stimulus developed in response to the coronavirus disease 20 19 
crisis , namely  job creation and ecosyst em restoration  
33. The p roject will continue to seek additional financing opportunities  from private and 
public sector funding  during its implementation . The AE will develop  a scaling up and 
replication strategy during implementation to continue expanding the Recharge Pakistan 
initiative to the rest of the country. The AE  will be requested to report on the potential 
leveraged private finance.  
3.6 Efficiency and effectiveness                                                  Scale:  Medium  
34. The funding proposal package presents a high -quality and robust economic analysis 
with clear and well -referenced assumptions as well as a clear definition of scope and the 
counterfactual scenarios. The economic analysis contains an estimation of all the relevant 
economic value s from the project activities, which  are consistent with the information provided


---
*Page 172*
---

 
GCF/ B.36 /02/Add. 02/Rev.01  
Page 172 
 
 
in the funding proposal. The model uses a social discount rate of 9.12 per cent , which has been 
computed as the average of the deposit interest rate in Pakistan in 2020 (7.5 per cent) and the 
rate of the country’s commercial bank s (10.6 per cent). The rate used also follows the approved 
FAO project (FP108) in the GCF portfolio. In summary, the economic model results show the net 
present value as USD 30 million and the economic  internal rate of return  as 13.6  per cent, 
indicatin g that the proposed project is economically viable.  
35. Financial adequacy and the appropriate ness  of concessionality is assessed to be high. 
From the supply side, banks and financial institutions in the targ et areas of Pakistan are 
currently not providing loans to smallholder farmers owing to the risks associated with 
uncertainty in profitability. From the demand side, smallholder farmers (around 63–85 per cent of farmers in Pakistan) have limited financial l iteracy, collateral and credit history,  and thus 
very limited access to finance in general. To address this issue, a strong business case is needed to ease financial risks, and the proposal is requesting grant funding from GCF to showcase such 
a business case. The justification is deemed satisfactory.  
36. The need for grants in this project is also justified by the public nature of project 
activities. In summary, 95.9  per cent  of the project costs are for public goods (e.g. rehabilitation 
of degraded lands for flood risk reduction). For o utput 3.1 ( pipeline development for climate-
resilient businesses), GCF resources are to be used for technical assistance and capacity -
building  for potential businesses. For output 3.2 ( climate -resilient agriculture), 84 per cent of 
funding is for techn ical assistance and training. The remaining USD 222,600 is for climate- smart 
agriculture inputs and water saving schemes (irrigation and water sto rage tanks) but the size of 
the funding was argued to be too small to conduct a formal financial model.  
37. The cost -effectiveness  of the project  is assessed to be medium. The project’s adaptation 
cost is USD 111/direct beneficiary and USD 10/total beneficiary . Cost per direct beneficiary is in 
the high range of the GCF portfolio for climate resilience in agriculture, compared with the other 
GCF -approved projects in the portfolio in Pakistan. Although the project is targeting adaptation 
results areas only, the AE estimated mitigation co -benefit s from carbon sequestration through 
forest afforestation, reforestation and a ssisted natural r egeneration. If the total GCF funding 
amount is regarded as mitigation cost, the cost  per t CO 2 eq would then be USD 73.22/t CO 2 eq 
over the 40 -year lifetime, which is within the price range of USD 40 –80/t  CO 2 eq to meet the 
Paris Agreement target (World Bank, 2021).3 
38. The co -financing ratio is 1:0. 18, which is lower than the target under the  updated 
Strategic Plan for the GCF 2020 –2023. The AE has provided justification for the low co -
financing,  particularly as substantial government budget , once planned as cofinance, has been 
redirected towards emergency relief because of  the climate -related disasters and subsequent 
economic impacts in the country. T he proposal does not describe  the potential to leverage or 
mobiliz e additional financial resources, but the AE has indicated that it will implement 
mobilization strategies to ensure the sustainability of the project and the possible  scale -up of 
the project to different areas, if successful.  
IV. Assessment of consistency with GCF safeguards and policies  
4.1 Environmental and social safeguards  
39. Project background. The project aims to create integrated approaches to flood and 
drought risk manag ement in the Indus Basin in Pakistan to strengthen the climate resilience of 
the most vulnerable rural communities while enabling a paradigm shift towards Ecosystem -
 
3 “World Bank.  2021.  State and Trends of Carbon Pricing 2021.  © Washington, DC: World  Bank.   
    http://hdl.handle.net/10986/35620  License:  CC BY 3.0 IGO .”


---
*Page 173*
---

 
GCF/ B.36 /02/Add. 02/Rev.01  
Page 173 
 
 
Based Adaptation (EbA) in Pakistan. The project will restore 14,215 ha of degraded watersheds 
and 34 km of flow paths and channels as EbA interventions. Furthermore, it will deploy 127 
green infrastructure interventions tailored for specific locations in four project sites in the Indus 
Basin. The project is expected to have positive impact s on p eople and the environment. It will 
improve both the quality and quantity of water for irrigation and drinking purposes, augment 
water governance system, enhance flora and fauna, and facilitate the adoption of smart 
agriculture techniques. Ultimately the enduring effects of the project will lead to reinforced 
climate resilience and overall food and water security in the arid and semi -arid regions of 
Pakistan.  
40. Environmental and social risk category and safeguards instrument . The AE has 
classified the project medium environmental and social (E&S) risk, which is equivalent to Category B as per GCF’s environmental and social risk categorization and within the AE’s E&S 
risk accreditation le vel. Given that the project itself is an environmental and social enhancement 
project, the Secretariat agrees with this categorization while noting the challenges faced by the 
project related to conflict sensitivity in certain project sites and the changin g security context in 
the region. The project design and built -in safeguards have brought the overall risk level to 
moderate, including to mitigate the project’s security risk through operationalizing a Security Plan. As the detailed activities and their e xact locations are yet to be defined, the AE has 
prepared an environmental and social management framework (ESMF), which also includes a 
process framework (PF) to address restrictions on land use and access to resources, and an 
indigenous peoples planning framework (IPPF) to address requirements for activities that are 
located near or within indigenous peoples’ communities and territories. A stakeholder 
engagement plan (SEP) was prepared separately, which contains consultations conducted to 
date for the pro ject design and outlines a detail process for further stakeholder engagement 
during project implementation.   
41. Compliance with GCF environmental and social safeguards (ESS) standards. The 
paragraphs below provide a brief description of the project’s complia nce with ESS standards.  
42. ESS1: Assessment and management of environmental and social risks and 
impacts. The project is categorized as medium risk against the AE’s safeguards integrated 
policies and procedures. The ESMF provides general information about the  profiles of the 
project areas, the baseline information about the project socio -economic conditions, the 
archetypes of project interventions and a related methodological framework for their further screening and planning at the site level. While the current baseline information is derived from 
socio -economic, ecological, and biophysical data collected for the project’s initial scoping, the 
project will conduct environmental scoping studies and develop thorough environmental 
baselines, including indicators for assessing the biophysical impacts of the proposed 
interventions, during the project implementation. The ESMF identifies the project’s E&S risks 
assessed against the project’s safeguards screening criteria per each project component, with 
the general mi tigation measures and roles and responsibilities to bring the mitigation measures 
into practice specified. The ESMF provides the relevant tools and processes for further assessing the site -specific E&S risks and impacts, including the potential cumulative effects. If the 
screening reveals any adverse environmental or social impacts that may arise from the planned activity, an ESMP will be required before the initiation of the project activity and following the 
guidelines provided in the ESMF.  
43. The ESMF inclu des a conflict and security assessment to delineate the project’s conflict 
and security related risks and potential impacts. Given the complex security situation in the region, the ESMF identifies that two project sites (Chakkar Lehri and DI Khan) overlay with the 
conflict areas, which are considered by the AE as medium security risk zones. The project site in 
Chakkar Lehri is situated in Balochistan, the largest and most underdeveloped region, which has 
undergone inter -tribal conflicts and conflicts betwee n law enforcement agencies and the 
separatist movement. The assessment notes sporadic incidents of violence and attacks in DI


---
*Page 174*
---

 
GCF/ B.36 /02/Add. 02/Rev.01  
Page 174 
 
 
Khan and that the changing security situation in Afghanistan may embolden terrorist factions in 
Pakistan. The AE has devised a Sec urity Plan based on its institutional experience of working in 
conflict -prone settings and with local institutions and communities to establish credibility and 
install confidence. The Security Plan, as part of the ESMF, includes a set of mitigation measures, 
risk reduction techniques, and security protocols to be followed by the project to manage such 
conflict sensitivity, such as through the engagement with the security forces and law 
enforcement agencies, close collaboration with t ribal elders in the targ eted provinces to ensure 
the project’s legitimacy and seek their advice avoid becoming unintentionally part of any 
conflict. In the case of security threats, the project will pursue measures including immediate 
withdrawal from project areas where security threats are identified. In addition to the screening 
of conflict sensitivity risk that will take place at activity - or site -level during implementation, the 
AE is suggested to conduct the site -specific conflict sensitivity assessments to inform the 
detailed design, siting, and stakeholder engagements. The AE is also suggested to continuously 
monitor the effectiveness of the Security Plan to ensure robust safety and security 
arrangements are put in place. Where high security or other risks of the project ari se, the 
implementation of the ESMF and the project should be responsive to the changing conditions 
and the results of monitoring throughout the project’s lifecycle.  
44. ESS2: Labour and working conditions. While the ground activities are yet to be 
identified and specified during the project implementation, the project will require hiring and 
engagement of project staff, field workers and unskilled labour. The ESMF has identified potential risks of unsafe labour practices, inadequate occupational health and safety equipment, 
child labour, and workplace discrimination, pertaining to the construction works, such as 
building flood dispersal embankments, excavating flow paths, and desilting ponds to enhance 
the water -holding capacity, to be conducted as part of the EbA and green infrastructure 
interventions. Following the safeguard eligibility and impacts screening list to be applied at site -
level, project activities involving child labour, forced labour, sexual exploitation, or other forms 
of exploitation, will be s creened out as ineligible activities. The ESMF requires that the project 
implement all reasonable precautions to protect the health and safety of workers through the 
introduction of preventive and protective measures, and that all labour hired for the exca vation 
work be provided with proper safety gear, temporary housing, first aid training and the relevant 
health and safety related insurance cover. The project will hire labour from locals of the area 
and ensure the labour rights of project -employed workers  will be observed. Further screening 
on labour and working conditions will be conducted and detail mitigation measures will be 
addressed in the site -specific ESMPs.  
45. ESS3: Resource efficiency and pollution prevention.  The ESMF identifies that the 
works inv olved in the EbA and green instruction solutions, such as excavation of silt, debris, and 
other obstructions and pest use in afforestation can cause pollution . The misplacement and 
improper disposal of excavated material could lead to polluting the landsca pe and degrading the 
natural habitat. The ESMF requires a waste management plan to be formulated as part of the 
site-specific ESMP before the commencement of the relevant activities. The project will use the 
silt from the excavation work to rehabilitate an d reinforce the sides of the flow paths as part of 
the EbA solutions. In terms of potential pollution  caused by pest use, the project will exclude 
activities involving the procurement and/or use of pesticides and other chemicals specified as 
persistent org anic pollutants or hazardous chemicals. Although the project will not directly 
promote the procurement and use of pesticides, there are chances that the project 
interventions, such as riverine afforestation, re -vegetation, nursery raising, and the change i n 
agricultural practices, could lead to the procurement and use of pesticides. The ESMF includes a 
pest management plan that includes the guiding principles and a five- step approach on 
integrated pest management to be followed during project implementation . The site -specific 
E&S screening and ESMPs will further identify and address the project’s potential 
environmental impacts.


---
*Page 175*
---

 
GCF/ B.36 /02/Add. 02/Rev.01  
Page 175 
 
 
46. ESS4: Community health, safety and security. The project is expected to benefit the 
health and safety of the local communities by reducing the risk of water directly flooding their 
agricultural lands, assets, and infrastructure. However, local communities may be exposed to 
risks related to the construction works, which may affect water turbidity and water table levels 
and lead to pot ential reduction in fish catch. Some project activities may reduce the access to 
water for irrigation purposes, especially for communities living downstream of the Indus River. 
There is also the potential risk that the project may inadvertently exacerbate community 
conflicts due to the restriction of access to land or resources and due to the selection of project 
beneficiaries and the access to the project benefits. The project will mitigate such risks by 
preventing adverse impact on water quality and supply to affected communities; ensuring the 
proper design and safety of the project infrastructure; putting in place protective mechanisms 
for the use of hazardous materials; following disease prevention procedures; and implementing 
the project’s security plan  to ensure emergency preparedness and response. To avoid escalating 
or creating community conflicts, the project will further screen the risks and potential impacts on community health and safety at the site -level during implementation. Additionally, follo wing 
the process framework (PF) provided in the ESMF, affected communities will participate in the 
identification, design, implementation and monitoring of project activities and the related 
mitigation measures to prevent and minimize the project’s potential adverse impacts on 
communities. As the exact impacts of the project on community health, safety and security will 
be further identified at the site -level through the safeguards screening and during project 
implementation, the AE is suggested to ensure s ite-specific assessment and mitigation measures 
and the operation of the project’s PF and security plan will mutually reinforce one another.  
47. ESS5: Land acquisition and involuntary resettlement. The project will exclude 
activities that lead to private land  acquisition, physical displacement, and voluntary or 
involuntary relocation of people, including non -titled and migrant people. The ESMF identifies 
that the project activities may involve novel or rigorous restrictions on the use of or access to 
natural r esources, and that there may be some small areas of land acquisition, and that certain 
interventions, such as those pertaining to cleaning up the lake, could lead to changes in tenure arrangements, community -based property rights or customary rights to lan d, territories, and/ 
or resources. The AE has triggered its own standard on restriction of access and resettlement 
and developed a process framework (PF) to guarantee transparency and equity in the planning 
and implementation of project activities and to a void and minimize the project’s adverse 
impacts on the resource -dependent local communities and their livelihoods. The PF outlines the 
processes of engaging the local communities in the design, implementation and monitoring of 
the project; delineating the prospective negative impacts of the restriction on the encompassing 
communities, including any gender differences; defining the eligibility criteria of the 
economically displaced communities to receive compensation benefits and development 
assistance; iden tifying mitigation measures to assist any economically displaced people to 
restore and improve livelihoods; and setting out the participatory monitoring arrangements and a grievance redress procedure to resolve possible disputes on restrictions on land use  and 
access to resources pertaining to the project. The PF will work with the safeguards screening to 
identify the exact social impacts of the project and address such impacts through site -specific 
ESMPs or developing specific livelihood restoration plans (LRPs). The PF details the processes and mitigation measures required, including on how to calculate and allocate the compensation 
to the affected communities. In the case of insufficient budget for an LRP as agreed with local 
communities, the project will  redesign the specific activity/activities so that a LRP will no longer 
be needed , or the impact is lessened in a manner that a new LRP can be agreed upon.  
48. ESS6: Biodiversity conservation and sustainable management of living natural 
resources. The project  is expected to produce significant conservation benefits while 
harnessing biodiversity and ecosystem services to build climate resilience. It will exclude 
activities that could negatively affect areas of critical natural habitats or any breeding ground of 
known rare/endangered species, and activities that result in the loss of biodiversity, alteration


---
*Page 176*
---

 
GCF/ B.36 /02/Add. 02/Rev.01  
Page 176 
 
 
of the functioning of ecosystems, and introduction of new invasive alien species. The ESMF 
identifies four types of activities considered by the project that  can adversely affect biodiversity 
and the integrity of terrestrial or aquatic ecosystems, if not managed properly. That is, revegetation and reforestation may lead to decrease in the water availability downstream and 
exotic or non -native tree species bein g planted; the construction of retention areas or rain 
ponds may affect local biodiversity; while dredging can improve water quality in lakes and wetlands, to clean the sediment may lead to large disturbance to the lake/ wetland and that 
temporarily increa sed sediment concentrations can disturb aquatic life; and terracing may lead 
to habitat conversion and soil degradation, and can have impact on local hydrology and limiting water availability downstream. Further screening will be conducted at the site- leve l to screen 
out activities that may use genetically modified organisms or modern biotechnologies or their 
products; involve the procurement and/or use of pesticides and other chemicals specified as 
persistent organic pollutants or hazardous substances; or develop forest plantations. The AE is 
suggested to identify the potential impacts on biodiversity and natural resources per each 
archetype of the potential interventions and ensure that caution must be exercised in the 
design, selection, and implementation  of site -specific interventions. The analysis of the project 
invention should also be informed by the environmental baseline to be developed at each  site 
and be continuously monitored.  
49. GCF Indigenous Peoples Policy and ESS 7: Indigenous Peoples . Through screening 
undertaken in the context of the GCF Indigenous Peoples Policy, the AE has identified that there are indigenous peoples within the project sites. These include Mohana or Mallah communit ies in 
Machar  Lake  Bagri in Chak er Lehri and adj acent areas and  Kehal and Mores of Taunsa and DI 
Khan areas . The AE has prepared an i ndigenous peoples planning framework as part of the 
environmental and social management framework  with the intention of preparing indigenous 
peoples plans through a free, prior and informed consent process in partnership with the 
relevant communities. The AE noted that engagement has already been undertaken in these areas during the development of the funding proposal and  that at this stage there is broad 
support from triba l elders for participation in the project. The stakeholder engagement plan 
acknowledges the influence of tribal groups in the project areas and the need to engage with 
and seek the approval of tribal elders.  
50. While  planned activities could restore livelihoo ds, including for  the Mohana , 
descendants of the first inhabitants of the Indus Valley who live in one of the last remaining floating villages on Lake Manchar and are dependent on the lake for fishing, there is also the risk 
that project activities could r esult in restrict ed access of Mohana people to fishing . This will be 
determined once the project activity locations are finalized , which is expected  to be  in the first 
six months of project implementation. The indigenous peoples planning framework  notes that 
as many as 450  Mohana families  may be affected by the project  activities.  Tribal groups exert 
influence in the project areas and there have also been historical tribal conflicts, including over 
water resources, in DI Khan and Balochistan , altho ugh the funding proposal  documentation 
notes that this is less prevalent now and that the tribal elders have assured  the AE of their 
cooperation, including through letters of commitment. To mitigate these risks , especially  to 
Mohana  communities and overall,  the AE will implement free, prior and informed consent  
processes, taking into consideration the participation of women and men, and not implement in 
areas that will present adverse impacts to the local communities. The approval of tribal elder s 
will be s ought through their ji rgah system before the start of all project activities.  
51. In relation to local and indigenous knowledge systems , EbA should provide 
opportunities for the inclusion and promotion of local and indigenous knowledge. The bulk of 
the activi ties are currently dedicated to green infrastructure as opposed to promoting bottom -
up knowledge exchange,  although traditional knowledge of water management, cultural 
landscape systems such as rain -fed ponds, wells and the karez system, are acknowledged in 
addition to other traditional forms of water -sharing. The karez  system in particular is an ancient 
form of water management that allows communal water -sharing. One of the project activities


---
*Page 177*
---

 
GCF/ B.36 /02/Add. 02/Rev.01  
Page 177 
 
 
involves restoring the natural hydrology of a karez system. The bedrock of the karez lies in the 
sociocultural institutions behind it and the implementing bodies would need to also ensure the 
engagement of local stakeholders – in this system also known as shareholders – and the 
strengthening of the  accompanying  knowledge and value systems to ensure the sustainability  of 
the karez.   
52. In line with its roles and functions, the In digenous Peoples Advisory Group is available 
to provide advice to the AE  and EE, who are also encouraged to share emerging good practices 
and success stories with the  group . 
53. ESS8: Cultural heritage. The project will exclude activities that can negatively impact 
areas with cultural, historical, or transcendent values for individuals and communities. While no significant impact on the cultural heritage is expected from the project, the pre -feasibility study 
identifies possible location of historic sites in pr oject areas therefore the AE has triggered its 
own standard on cultural resources to safeguard the project’ potential impacts applicable to 
both physical and intangible cultural resources. The pre -feasibility study recommended  the 
need for further informat ion for the project site in DI Khan, where historical structures built on 
the Luni and Gomal River diversion point near to Kot Azam were completely  damaged during 
the super floods in 2010. More information is needed as to the extent of the damage and 
wheth er they continue to hold cultural significance regardless of the damage. The project site in 
the Manchar -Hamal Watershed, Lake Manchar, is also an important historical area. Home to 
several archaeological sites, there are likely intangible cultural resourc es for the Mohana people 
who have innumerable tangible and intangible cultural ties to the lake. An initial baseline 
information has been collected based on community surveys and field visits. Given the specific 
activities and locations of the project’s activities are yet to be determined, the ESMF provides a 
set of potential mitigation measures on cultural heritage, including to: conduct further baseline 
study and impact assessment on cultural heritage, as part of the landscape- level E&S screening 
and the efforts of identifying the i ndigenous peoples’ cultural heritage and complying with the 
regulatory requirements; prevent the negative impacts of the projects at the design stage to 
ensure that the planned activities do not harm the physical or cultural heritage; and develop a 
cultural heritage management plan prior to the implementation of project activities to preserve 
both tangible and intangible cultural heritage.  
54. Sexual exploitation, abuse and harassment (SEAH) . The GCF Revised Environmental  
and Social Policy adopted by decision B.BM -2021/18 requires safeguarding from SEAH in GCF -
financed activities. From a policy perspective, SEAH is grouped in the laws and terms of gender -
based violence  (GBV) , hono ur killings and domestic abuse. During gend er assessment 
consultations, the AE found that GBV in the country is alarming and noted that it has included 
some GBV/SEAH mitigation measures in the gender action plan. The AE has a zero -tolerance 
approach to GBV and acknowledges that some of its project interventions could potentially have 
a high risk  of GBV/SEAH owing  to power imbalances. It recognizes that  the achievement of 
biodiversity conservation is vital , while promoting gender equality , and make s every effort to 
ensure that project activities impl emented by WWF respect integrity and human rights and 
mitigate any risk that gives rise to discriminatory and exploitative gender inequalities. The 
guidance note on GBV and SEAH will assist project teams in identifying  any risks of GBV and 
SEAH that may em erge in conservation projects. Further more , this note will support decision -
making and inform planning and implementation of possible mitigation measures to address 
any such  risks identified during project preparation and execution. Where applicable, the 
project team may be required to prepare a detailed GBV/SEAH action plan and include any 
identified GBV/SEAH mitigation measures in project monitoring and reporting. The project management unit ( PMU ) will also facilitate the identification of any potential S EAH risks ; SEAH 
complaints handling ; and the provision of necessary training/capacity -building to project staff 
and implementation partners. GCF  recommends that the SEAH grievance and reporting 
mechanisms be survivor -cent red and gender -responsive and that modalities be put in place that 
will provide timely services and redress to survivors, including , as appropriate, medical care,


---
*Page 178*
---

 
GCF/ B.36 /02/Add. 02/Rev.01  
Page 178 
 
 
psychosocial sup port, legal support, community -driven protection measures and reintegration 
where applicable.  
55. Implementation arrangements. The ESMF describes the management structure and 
the roles and responsibilities related to project implementation and safeguards imple mentation. 
While the AE is responsible for ensuring both the GCF and AE’s own policy requirements and 
standards are applied and met, WWF Pakistan, as the EE, will be responsible for the 
implementation of all project components and activities, including ensuring the implementation 
of and compliance with the ES MF. A Project Management Unit (PMU), comprised of safeguards -
related senior management including an E&S safeguards manager and a gender manager, will 
supervise the implementation of the ESMF and other s afeguard plans at the site/landscape 
levels. It will also be responsible for the implementation . The PMU will be accountable to a 
Project Steering Committee (PSC), which will be established to provide technical guidance to the 
PMU and ensure alignment amon g the PMU with national, provincial and project -site level 
entities. The PSC will oversee the project’s compliance with the safeguards commitments and 
provide recommendations on specific safeguards issues where needed. In addition to the E&S 
safeguards man ager, the project will also deploy three safeguards staff at the site -level 
responsible for the on -the-ground implementation of the ESMF and related plans once they 
have been finalized.  
56. Stakeholder engagement and information disclosure. The project underwe nt 
extensive consultations with key stakeholders respectively during the project design phase and 
during the preparation of the ESMF. Workshops, stakeholder meetings, and field -level 
consultations with local stakeholders and community groups were organized , and 
complemented by site visits, field inspections, and focus group discussions. The consultations 
during the ESMF preparation focused on identifying the potential adverse impacts of the project 
with mitigation options as well as assessing the potential project benefits and how these can be 
enhanced in favour of the local communities. It is concluded by the AE that the project affected 
communities were supportive of the planned project activities. A SEP has been prepared which 
summarizes the process and r esults of the consultations conducted so far and provides a 
detailed plan for further stakeholder engagement during the project’s implementation phase, 
covering the project’s executing partners and stakeholders, including non -government 
stakeholders, to be further engaged at the national, provincial, and local levels. The ESMF 
requires that all affected communities and relevant stakeholders be informed about the ESMF requirements and commitments, and that the project’s safeguards documentation, including 
site-specific ESMPs, will be disclosed to all affected communities and the public following a 
disclosure framework specified in the ESMF.  
57. Grievance redress mechanism (GRM). The ESMF provides the principles for the 
development of an activity -level GRM that w ill be finalized with training provided to the 
project’s safeguards personnels within the first six months of the project implementation. The 
project -level GRM will be operationalized, in addition to and independently from the EE’s own 
internal GRM system,  following a separate structure, process flow, and hierarchy as outlined in 
the ESMF, which will enable all complaints going to straight to the PMU and with a whistle-
blower mechanism to allow project communities to raise complaints anonymously. In additio n 
to the project specific GRM, a complainant can submit a grievance to the AE’s independent, third -party GRM as well as to the project complaints officer, who is fully independent from the 
project team, hosted by the AE. The project will provide informatio n of the GRMs available to 
the project, including GCF’s Independent Redress Mechanism as an integral, into the community consultations. All the consultation sessions will be conducted in the local language of the area, 
and the local communities will also r eceive training on how to file and lodge complaints.  In line 
with the GCF Indigenous Peoples Policy, the GCF indigenous peoples focal point will be available 
for assistance at any stage, including before a claim has been made.

4.2 Gender policy  
58. The AE provided a gender assessment and action plan with the funding proposal,  
there by compl ying  with the requirements of the GCF Gender Policy.  
59. The status of women in Pakistan over the last three decades has been fluctuating and 
has seen many structural factors/changes over time. The exi sting numerous laws put in place 
provisions that will ensure women’s rights and protection, but the implementation of the same 
and lack of political will is still a challenge. Women’s participation in decision -making proce sses 
remains a challenge, and formal institutions , including private and public institutions , fall short 
of facilitating women’s access to financial  resources  education, skills, health facilities , etc. 
Informal/community institutions,  including religious spaces , define and reinforce child 
marriages and gender roles at the household and/or community level. The Global Database on 
Violence against W omen show s that 24.5  per cent of women  experienced lifetime physical 
and/or sexual intimate partner violence betw een 2017 and 2018. The impact patterns are also 
different for women and men. A s hrinking natural resource base has led to migration and shifts 
in livelihoods and roles, and there is a need for alternative livelihoods.   
60. The gender action plan for the Recha rge Pakistan project was developed on the bas is of 
the results and recommendations from the gender assessment , outlining gender -specific actions 
against each activity that aim to ensure that key gender issues are adequately incorporated in 
the overall project design and that stand -alone gender activities are carried out. The gender 
specialist/manager based at the PMU will lead the implementation of the gender action pla n and 
facilitate various  capacity needs assessments and training at the onset of the project 
implementation phase. The AE will also coordinate (on other outputs) with the National 
Commission on the S tatus of Women, the Women Development Department, the Council on 
Gender Equality, vocational training institutions and government agencies and line departments 
at the national and subnational level working on gender equality and women’s empowerment. 
The gender -specific activities that will be implemented under outcome 1 include (a) int egrating 
gender dimensions in to the integrated floods and d rought management Tool; ( b) procurement 
of plant saplings from nurseries raised by local women; ( c) formation of women’s groups on 
natural resources, water, climate change and environment; ( d) capa city-building of local women 
and men to understand what climate change is, building their leadership skills so that they will be able to actively engage in the CBO meetings;  and (e) holding consultative meetings with CBO 
women ’s groups on operation and maintenance (O&M) of green infrastructure. Under outcome 
2 the AE will ( a) ensure that gender considerations are included in the resource management 
regulatory frameworks  during the development of policy briefs on EbA intervention s; (b) 
sensitize relevant government line departments on gender and women’s empowerment in EbA 
across their planning and policy processes ; and (c) The AE will set up a Project Steering 
Committee and p rovincial oversight committees , which will  facilitate mainstreaming gender in 
initiatives and policies that focus on improving natural resource management through EbA and 
similar programmes. Finally, under  outcome 3, the AE will ( a) ensure that women and men have 
access to capacity -building workshops focused on  bankable projects/green financing ; (b) 
ensure that women and men are trained on climate -resilient agriculture practices covering both 
drought and flooding scenarios ; and ( c) ensure that gender issues are mainstreamed in the 
implementation of climate- resil ient agriculture interventions , including  through high ly efficient 
irrigation systems, kitchen gardening, fruit orchards, fodder cultivation and water storage 
tanks ; (d) build  the technical capacity of local women on alternative livelihoods and 
entrepreneurship ; and (e) implement gender -specific diversified income -generati ng activities 
including, but not limited to, fruit and vegetable processing units (packaging, labelling and pos t-
harvest processing along with value addition), handicrafts, poultry farming and apiculture.

4.3.3.  Programme -specific execution risks (medium risk)

responsibilities of the relevant government departments for the required O&M activity. This willbe further elaborated, including financing arrangement s and budgetary allocation , during the
implementation period. Given the current budgetary constraints , the government ’s capacity to
finance O&M cost beyond the project implementation is uncertain and the current commitment
assumed that there would still be interest from the relevant ministries/governments. At th
e
c
ommunity level, the relevant provincial d epartments and CBOs will establish agreements o n
co
ntinuous monitoring and management of the green infrastructure and ecosystem protection.
The AE mentioned that the CBOs will work with a w ater user group to explore potential options
for fee structures for  irrigation and drinking water which may support the O&M. Another GCF -
financed project (FP108) in Pakistan is working on water tariff matters , which could serve as a
basis for the CBOs/ water user groups  to develop fee structures.
66. Foreign exchange rate  risk: the AE identified foreign exchange rate risk as one of th e
r
isks which could affect the financial performance of the project such as over spending  or
underspending of the budget. The country has experienced significant local currency fluctuation
(from –30 to +10 per cent) in the past three years and its foreign exchange reserves hav e fallen
to very low levels according to  the information provided by the external service provider i n
M
arch 2023. In the case of significant exchange gain or loss, the AE should immediately inform
GCF , which  will take appropriate action by mutual agreement with the AE within the B oard -
approved financing amount. Moreover , the project is financed by full grant without government
budgetary support, which will be less influenced by any repayment obligation and fisc al

ituation of the government.

67. The Dutch Fund for Climate and Dev elopment ( DFCD ) support projects (pre -identified): 
GCF proceeds under component 3 will be used to support projects pre -identified by DFCD. Since 
the preparatory funding from DFCD is over, the GCF proceeds will fill up the gap to allow these 
projects to be bankable and find investors. Mobiliz ing funds to cover the capital expenditure wi ll 
be the key for the actual investment to materialize on the ground. The funding proposal includes 
an indicator of the number of signed agreements to ensure this.   
68. Economic analysis and project viability: the AE carried out an economic analysis, which 
result ed in an economic internal rate of return  of 13  per cent  over 30 years. The analysis 
considers the benefits from the avoided damage, cost savings in agriculture and carbon sequestration. Carbon sequestration is the greatest benefit derived from Eb A and green 
infrastructure. The analysis includes a sensitivity testing with changes of inputs in underlying assumptions such as the low and high shadow price of carbon. The assumptions and referenced 
data used for analysis are deemed to be robust and justify the request for the grant. As the 
analysis assumed that the cost of O&M  will continue  beyond the project implementation, the 
long- term sustainability and viability of the project will depend on the capacity of the 
government and local communities to mobilize the resources to cover the O&M cost.   
4.3.4.  Compliance risk ( medium risk)   
69. Pakistan is not currently subject to any United Nations Security Council (UNSC) 
resolutions  (i.e. sanctions). 
70. WWF -US, as the AE for the project, confirmed that no persons or entities listed on any 
UNSC  sanctions list, including the UN SC Consolidated Sanctions list , will be involved in any 
manner with the project or its activities, either as a counterparty, implementer or beneficiary.  
71. The AE confirmed that it has conducted a risk assessment concerning prohibited 
practices – that is,  corruption, fraud or abuse lead ing to theft, misappropriation, waste or 
improper use of property, assets or GCF funding. The probability of risk occurrence was 
assessed as low, and impac t severity has been assessed as medium.  
72. The AE confirmed that it has also conducted a risk assessment concerning money -
laundering and terrorist financing – i.e. improper use of property, assets or GCF funding , as well 
as p roject actors that fail to comply with all applicable laws on anti -money -laundering and 
countering the financing of terrorism  (AML/CFT) . The probability of risk occurrence was 
assessed as low, and impact severity has been assessed as medium. Th e risk assessment:  
(a) Verified the identity of EE ; 
(b) Checked EE against a series of watch lists using LexisNexis Bridger Insight s oftware;  
(c) Assessed the purpose, economic rationale and overall risks associated with the recipient on the basis of all relevant information, including, for example, the type of t hird parties 
involved and the financial instrument proposed by the third parties; and 
(d) Includes the legal and financial due diligence assessments completed on EE.  
73. The AE  confirmed that  GCF funds will be administered by WWF- Pakistan. WWF -
Pakistan will be required to agree, in writing, not to engage in transactions with, or provide 
resources or support to, individuals and organizations associated with terrorism, including those individuals or entities that appear on the Specially Designated Nationals and Blocked 
Persons List maintained by the U nited States  Department of the Treasury

79. The AE indicated  that the due diligence process typically involves the collection and 
analysis of information about beneficiaries and downstream partners. This information 
includes , but is not limited to, their identity, background, financial status, accounting standards, 
cash handling procedures, safeguarding of assets and legal history. Such information is gathered 
through various means, including the examination of publicly avail able records, conducting 
interviews and making site visits. The purpose of due diligence is to identify any potential red flags or risks associated with the beneficiaries and downstream partners, such as involvement 
in illegal activities, financial instability, conflict of interest or reputational issues.  
80. The AE confirmed that, o ther than those entities included in the implementation 
arrangements which will be subject to due diligence  processes, there will be no  intention to 
distribute or disburse , directly  or indirectly, cash vouchers, commodities or other items of value 
to any other beneficiaries.   
81. The AE confirmed that several mechanisms will  be available for the reporting of 
complaints and allegations of impropriety, wrong -doing or other related issues in the project 
and its activities (i.e. whistle -blower program mes). The WWF whistle -blower system is open to 
staff, partners, communities and other stakeholders to report suspected illegal or inappropriate 
activity, or concerns about the implementation of WWF projects. This system is in the form of an 
online and tele phone mechanism hosted by a third -party provider, WhistleB, which can receive 
reports online or by tele phone in multiple languages. All complaints submitted through the 
mechanism are investigate d.

4.5 Results monitoring and reporting  
91. The funding proposal  is an adaptation project focusing on two result s areas of the 
Integrated Results Management Framework : (i) m ost vulnerable people and communities and 
(ii) ecosystems and ecosystem services. Through key interventions in EbA, green infrastructure 
and community -based adaptation in select ed watershed areas of Pakistan, the project aims to 
benefit a total of 7.7 million individuals,  with 0.7 million direct and 7.0 million indirect 
beneficiaries'  adaptation interventions .  
92. The FP has been found to use the templates and provide contents in line with the 
Integrated Results Management Framework  requirements. The sections on T heory of Change  
and logical frame work  have been aligned with the suggested structure and format. A continuous 
feedback mechanism has enabled refinement of the elements of the log ical frame work  in terms 
of identification of result s areas, indicators, means of verification, targets, assumptions, etc. 
They are found to be adequate and are expected to allow smooth monitoring and reporting of project results. The information provided in different sections of the log ical frame work  are 
clearly articulated ; their proper implementation will help to yield adaptation mechanisms, tools 
and practices for the communities in the country.  
93. The assessment of adaptation benefits and beneficiar ies is a strength of the 
funding pro posal.  Following the Secretariat’s guidance, a separate annex on a methodology for 
the estimation of adaptation beneficiar ies have been prepared , which provides a description of 
vulnerability context, identification of adaptation benefits and related beneficiaries. The direct 
and indirect beneficiaries are distinguished under a sound assumption. The methodology has 
allowed a systematic estimation of adaptation beneficiaries , which will help in devising 
approaches to reach them during implementation, as well as monitoring and re porting on 
adaptation beneficiaries.  
94. Implementation timetable : the implementation timetable has been provided in the 
required structure and format. The milestones and deliverables that are envisaged at different stages of project implementation are adequat ely planned. Adherence to the proposed 
implementation timetable will facilitate smooth implementation of the project as well as timely 
delivery of the results at planned intervals.  
4.6 Legal assessment  
95. The Accreditation Master Agreement was signed with the AE on 16 November 2017, and 
was amended and restated by the first amendment and restatement agreement on 12 December 
2022 which became effective on 13 December 2022 (as so amended and restated, the “ AMA ”). 
96. The AE has not provided a legal opinion/certificate c onfirming that it has obtained all 
internal approvals and has the capacity and authority to implement the project. It is 
recommended that, prior to submission of the funding proposal to the Board (a) the AE has 
obtained all its internal approvals and (b) t he Fund has received a certificate or legal opinion 
from the AE in form and substance satisfactory to the Fund confirming that all final internal approvals by the AE have been obtained and that it has the authority and capacity to implement 
the project.  
97. The proposed project will be implemented in Pakistan, a country in which GCF is not 
provided with privileges and immunities. This means that, among other things, GCF is not 
protected against litigation or expropriation in this country, which risks need to b e further


---
*Page 185*
---

 
GCF/ B.36 /02/Add. 02/Rev.01  
Page 185 
 
 
assessed. GCF provided a draft agreement on privileges and immunities, along with a 
background note, to Pakistan on 15 September 2015; however, Pakistan has yet not indicated 
that it is ready to start the negotiations on the agreement.  
98. The Heads  of the Independent Redress Mechanism and the Independent Integrity Unit 
have both expressed that it would not be legally feasible to undertake their redress activities 
and/or investigations, as appropriate, in countries where GCF is not provided with relevant 
privileges and immunities. Therefore, it is recommended that disbursements by GCF are made 
only after GCF has obtained satisfactory protection against litigation and expropriation in the 
country, or has been provided with appropriate privileges and im munities.  
99. Pursuant to clause 4.05(b) of the AMA, the AE is required to perform, among other 
things, legal due diligence on the proposed project. This legal due diligence is to be carried out 
by the AE for the purposes of developing the funding proposal and , when reviewing the funding 
proposal, GCF will rely on such due diligence (see decision B.12/31, annex XXVII, chapter II). The Secretariat notes that the AE’s legal due diligence for the project (see annex 9 to the funding 
proposal), has revealed that the agreement between the Executing Entity and the Government 
of Pakistan, pursuant to which the Executing Entity was authorized to work in Pakistan, has 
expired on 5 August 2021, and that it has not been renewed.  The AE’s due diligence further 
revealed that  the application to renew was still underway and the Executing Entity was in 
continuous contact with relevant authorities.  To address implementation risks associated with 
the foregoing, a condition precedent to the execution of the funded activity agreement has been 
inserted into the term sheet according to which the AE shall deliver to GCF Secretariat a 
certificate or a legal opinion confirming that the Executing Entity is authorized to implement the 
project in Pakistan .

Independent Technical Advisory Panel’s assessment of 
FP207 
Proposal name:  Recharge Pakistan: Building Pakistan’s resilience to climate 
change through Ecosystem- based Adaptation (EbA) and Green 
Infrastructure for integrated flood risk management  
Accredited entity:  World Wildlife Fund, Inc. (WWF)  
Country/(ies):  Pakistan  
Project/programme size: Medium 
I. Assessment of the independent Technical Advisory Panel
1.1 Overview  
1. The proposed project  will take place in Pakistan, a country characterized by a diversit y
o
f ecosystems, climate zones and landscapes  (approximately 60 per cent  rough mountainous
terrain and plateaus, and 40 per cent  level plain) , and a wide variety of natural resources. With
an economy long based on agr iculture, Pakistan is now considered a semi -industrialized
country  facing considerable challenges in balancing the competing objectives of socioeconomi c
d
evelopment and environmental protection. The number s of people living below the poverty
line (2013 dat a) and malnourished  (2018 data)  are estimated at 29.5  per cent  and 12.3 per cent ,
r
espectively. Most of Pakistan ’s population, estimated at 225.1  million, live in the  Indus River
basin, an area highly  exposed to severe floods in July and August.
2. The proposed project  targets two catchments  of the Indus River  basin , with
interventions at four sites, namely  Dera Ismail (DI)  Khan and Ramak located in  the DI Khan
catchment , and Chakar Lehri and Manchar loca ted in  the Chakar Lehri catchment. Situated
respectively in the Khyber Pakhtunkhwa province, and the Balochistan and Sindh provinces , the
DI
 Khan and Chakar Lehri  catchments have been selected because  their ecosystems and
communit ies are highly vulnerable to the impacts of climate change, and because there ar e
op
portunit ies for adaptation benefits through e cosystem- based adaptation (EbA) and green
infrastructure interventions. Agriculture is the main resource for the subsistence of local peop le
i
n the selec ted sites .
3. The total budget required to implement the project over its 7 -year duration is USD
77,848,396 , including USD 66,000,005  in the form of a grant from the GCF . The remainder will
consist of USD 10 million from United States Agency for International Development  (USAID ) and
the Coca -Cola Foundation , as well as an in -kind contribution from WWF- Pakistan  estimated a t
U
SD 1,848,391 .
4. Pakistan’ s vulnerability to climate change is largely due to its geographical location and
socioeconomic conditions :
(a) Pakistan is located in a semi -arid to mos tly arid region prone to recurrent large -scale
flo
ods, earthquakes, droughts and landslides. Its economy, notably the agricultural and
hydroelectric sectors, is based on the development of water resources from rivers and
glacial melt, which are highly dependent on climatic conditions.  In recent years, th e
c
ountry ’s climate has changed, with rising temperatures and more variable rainfall ,
c
ompromising not only the functioning of the above -men tioned economic sectors, but


---
*Page 187*
---

 
GCF/ B.36 /02/Add. 02/Rev.01  
Page 187 
 
 
also causing considerable loss and damage to Pakistan society as a whole. The major 
floods of 2010 and 2022 and their imp acts are the most recent examples. With the 
climate conditions anticipated for the country  – particularly an average temperature  
increase  of up to 3.7  °C by 2060 and up to 6  °C by 2090, a decrease in the volume of 
glaciers and snow cover, and especially an increase in the frequency and intensity of 
extreme and slow -onset events  – the situation is set to worsen if nothing is done to 
sustainably address these adverse climatic conditions, including major floods and 
droughts ; and  
(b) The a daptive capacity  of Pakistani stakeholders to adverse climatic conditions 
attributable to climate change is greatly co mpromised by a number  of unfavourable  
socioeconomic situations. Indeed, the country faces relatively high levels of malnutrition and of people living below the poverty line, and contains communities and groups 
marginalized by economic status, location and political circumstances. In addition, 
complex institutional context of Pakistan makes it difficult to adopt sustainable 
solutions to the challenges facing the country.  
5. The proposal’s the ory of change  states that “If the interventions envisaged by the 
fundi ng proposal (EbA and green infrastructure)  are integrated into the country ’s planning 
systems and into future adaptation actions, then  the targeted communities will be less 
vulnerable to floods and droughts, and governments at different levels will have th e planning 
tools in hand to support and operationalize the interventions because  the main barriers, 
notably the lack of proofs of concepts for EbA and green infrastructure as viable adaptation 
intervention in Pakistan, the inadequate technical capacity acr oss government and communities 
for implementing EbA and green infrastructure intervention in Pakistan, and the limited access to finance among the most vulnerable communities and micro -, small - and medium size 
enterprises for climate -resilient, sustainable  businesses, will have been removed.”  
6. The main objective of the proposed project is to address climate change- induced floods 
and droughts in a sustainable way, using an anticipatory approach that combines EbA,  green 
and green -grey infrastructures.  To achieve this objective , the proposed project is built around 
three main components: component 1: p roofs of concept for EbA and green  and green -grey  
infrastructure interventions as efficient and effective solutions to flood and drought risk 
reduction in Pakistan; component 2: e nabling a paradigm shift towards EbA and green  and 
green -grey  infrastructure in Pakistan ; and component 3: e nhanced community resilience and 
adoption of EbA  and green  and green -grey  infrastructure interventions in Pakistan’s Indus 
basin. These components are expected to deliver the following results : outcome 1:  EbA and 
green  and green -grey  infrastructure interventions with improved community -led management 
will reduce flood risk and enhance the climate resilience of the most vulnerable people in 
Pakistan’s Indus b asin ; outcome 2: the development of an enabling environment for climate 
action that has new procedures for implementing EbA and green  and green -grey  infrastructure 
interventions for flood and water resources management in Pakistan ; and o utcome 3: increased 
resilience of community livelihoods in the Indus b asin.  
7. The World Wildlife Fund, Inc. (WWF -US) will serve as the a ccredited entity (AE) for the 
project  and WWF -Pakistan  will be the executing entity , which will establish and host the p roject 
management unit within WWF -Pakistan’s office in the Federal Capital, Islamabad . Furthermore, 
there will be four p rovincial implementation units hosted within WWF -Pakistan’s regional 
offices located in the p rovincial capitals (Khyber Pakhtunkhwa, Sindh and Balochistan) , and s ite 
implementation units will be based at  field offices in the following locations: ( i) DI Khan 
Watershed , covering  the DI Khan and Ramak Watershed project areas; ( ii) Chakar Lehri 
Watershed; and ( iii) Manchar Lake Watershed.  
1.2 Impact potential                 Scale: Medium  to high


---
*Page 188*
---

 
GCF/ B.36 /02/Add. 02/Rev.01  
Page 188 
 
 
8. The p roject inter ventions will directly benefit a n estimated  total of 687,336 people, 
including 357,414 men (52  per cent  of the total population  of the region ) and 329,922 women 
(48 per cent  of the total population  ), as well as indirectly benefit ing 7,024,361 people, incl uding 
3,652,668 men (52  per cent  of total population) and 3,371,693 women (48  per cent  of total 
population) in Pakistan’s Indus b asin.  
9. By strengthening the adaptive capacity of institutions and stakeholders at different 
administrative levels, the interventions promoted by the project are likely to be implemented 
and consequently have positive impacts in the management of large -scale floods and droughts 
in the country.  This will be achieved through  interventions on the ground in the four project 
sites to redirect and contain the flow of water,  in order to minimize downstream flash floods 
and to facilitate infiltration to replenish groundwater resources  and help address droughts. 
These interventions  includ e EbA interventions such as  restoration, recon nection and/or 
rehabilitation of watersheds, floodplains and wetlands; and green  (and green -grey)  
infrastructure interventions such as retention areas and recharge basins . Community -based 
adaptation interventions  will suppor t vulnerable smallholder farmers  to adopt drought -
resilient crop varieties, to increase their capacity for resilient and sustainable irrigation and soil 
management, and to enhance irrigation through new water storage tanks.  
10. The proposed project should also contribute to changes in the re gulatory and 
procedural arsenal for flood and drought management in Pakistan , to include  climate -resilient 
land and water resources management strategies (e.g. expanding flood and water resources 
regulations and policies to prioriti ze EbA and green  and green -grey  infrastructure 
interventions).  The implementation of the regulations and procedures developed under the 
project should contribute to ensuring  that  the proposed interventions are adopted and 
consequently contribute to positive impacts on the  socio -ecological systems facing the 
socioeconomic development challenges posed by floods and droughts in the targeted regions.  
11. Adaptation impact potential is rated as  Medium to High. 
1.3 Paradigm shift potential               Scale: Medium  to high 
12. The proposed project aims to bring about a change in approach to planning for the 
management of major floods and droughts in Pakistan. Building on the positive results already 
achieved through the implementation of the EbA and green infrastructure intervention s in other 
regions of the world , it intends to foster an anticipatory and integrated approach to the 
management of the above -mentioned climate risks, combining both EbA and green and green -
grey infrastructure  interventions . In addition, to facilitate this change in the approach of the 
Government of Pakistan and various actors at the departmental level, the proposed project 
envisage s setting up a new coordination structure , the National Working Group . Chaired by the 
Ministry of Climate Change and composed of  regulators within the Ministry of Water Resources  
and its e xecutive department, the Federal Flood Commission , as well as the relevant p rovincial 
departments , this National Working Group will lead the development and adoption of the 
procedures  integrating the interventions promoted by the project.  
13. The proposed project will create an enabling environment for the long -term adoption of 
flood and drought management interventions in Pakistan. Indeed, it will be able to provide 
evidence on the efficiency and effectiveness of EbA and green infrastructure interventions, and 
promote reforms in the planning process and national and provincial policies for large -scale 
flood and drought management in Pakistan. In addition, the project will contribute to building 
community capacity on the issue. All this is likely to encourage the adoption of the proposed 
interventions beyond the project ’s duration.  
14. The proposed project has a high potential for scaling up and replication. It will serve as 
the key proof point in Pakistan t hat EbA and green infrastructure interventions are cost -


---
*Page 189*
---

 
GCF/ B.36 /02/Add. 02/Rev.01  
Page 189 
 
 
effective and impactful adaptation options. The G overnment of Pakistan  has already committed 
to expanding EbA and green infrastructure beyond the GCF project’s four  sites to five  additional 
sites in Haleji, Hadero and Nara -Deh Akro Watersheds in Sindh Province, Indus Dolphin Reserve 
in Sindh Province, Isa Khel Watershed in K hyber Pakhtunkhwa, and Lala Creek in Punjab 
Province . 
15. It is important to note that the potential for paradigm shift is linked to the proposed 
project ’s ability to establish robust evidence of the efficiency and effectiveness of its 
interventions.  
16. Paradigm shift  potential of the proposed project is rated as M edium to H igh.  
1.4 Sustainable development potential              Scale: Medium  to high

namely SDG 1 on poverty,  SDG 2 on zero hunger; SDG 5 on g ender equality, SDG 14 on l ife below 
water and SDG 1 5 on life on l and.

19. It is important to highlight that the sustainable development potential of the proposed 
project is also closely linked to the efficiency and effectiveness of the selected interventions.  
20. Sustainable development po tential  is rated as Medium  to High . 
1.5 Needs of the recipient               Scale: High  
21. Pakistan is very exposed and vulnerable to floods and droughts , and it is ranked as the 
eighth most affected country in the world on the German Watch Long -Term Climate Risk Index 
(2000 –2019) . The exceptional floods of 2010 and 2022 clearly demonstrated the country ’s


---
*Page 191*
---

 
GCF/ B.36 /02/Add. 02/Rev.01  
Page 191 
 
 
great vulnerability to these extreme, slow -onset climatic events, which are set to intensify with 
climate change. The aforementioned floods caused considerable material and human damage, 
forcing the country to devote its meagre resources to repairing the damage caused. For 
example, the G overnment of Pakistan is no longer in a position to provide the co -financing 
originally envisaged for the proposed project, as resources have been diverted to emergencies 
linked to the major floods of 2022.  
22. Pakistan ’s adaptation needs, as well as those related to loss and damage caused by 
adverse weather conditions induced by climate change, are enormous. They are estimated at 
several billion U nited States  dollars. Al though Pakistan, one of the first countries in Asia to 
create a Ministry of Climate Change, has funds in the national budget for adaptation actions, it is 
unable to meet the immense needs in this field because of the urgent actions required by other 
develo pment initiatives. In addition, the country ’s high level of indebtedness, its economic 
development challenges and the national emergency resulting from the impacts of climate 
change mean that it can only provide limited financial support for adaptation act ions. As a 
result, the  country is  obliged to turn to international public funding sources such as the Green 
Climate Fund.  
23. Although climate change information and adaptation responses such as EbA  exist, 
Pakistan ’s water resources sector is still unable to integrate them into its planning processes, 
making it even more vulnerable to floods and droughts. This lack of integration of climate 
change issues into water resources management is due in particular to a weak institutional 
capacity to plan and implement adaptation actions. The proposed project intends to address 
these shortcomings /gaps  by implementing activities such as EbA, and green  and green -grey  
infrastructure  in the project sites, and by improving the enabling environment to scale up such 
activities elsewhere in the country .  
24. Needs of the recipients is rated  as High.  
1.6 Country ownership               Scale: High  
25. The pro posed project  contributes to a set of objectives and priorities defined in the 
National Cli mate Change Policy, the framework document for addressing the current and future 
climate change challenges facing Pakistan. Indeed, the entire  proposed project contributes to 
the objective of pursuing sustained economic growth by appropriately addressing t he challenge 
of climate change. More specifically, o utputs 2.1 and 2.2 contribute to the objective of 
integrating climate policy with other interrelated national policies; outputs 1.1, 1.2, 1.3, 3.1 and 
3.2 contribute to the objective of focusing on pro- poor sensitive adaptation; outputs 1.1, 1.2, 1.3 
and 3.2 contribute to the objective of ensuring water, food and energy security for the country 
in the face of challenges posed by climate change; outputs 1.1, 1.2, 1.3, 2.1, 2.2, 2.3 and 3.2 
contribute to the  objective of minimizing the risks arising from the expected increase in 
frequency and intensity of extreme weather events such as floods, droughts and tropical storms; 
outputs 2.1 , 2.2 and 2.3 contribute to the objective of strengthening interministerial decision -
making and coordination mechanisms on climate change; and output 3.1 contributes to the 
objective of facilitating effective use of the opportunities, particularly financial, available both 
nationally and internationally. The proposed project is al so in line with the National Forest 
Policy, the National Wetlands Policy, the National Disaster Risk Management Framework, the 
National Disaster Risk Reduction Policy, and the updated n ationally determined contribution.  
26. An extensive and inclusive stakeholder consultation process was used to formulate the 
proposed project . These stakeholders included the Ministry of Climate Change, representatives 
from national and subnational government such as the Ministry of Water Resources ( including 
the Federal Flood Commission ), the National Disaster Management Authority and the Ministry 
of Planning Development and Special Initiatives ; provincial government departments covering


---
*Page 192*
---

 
GCF/ B.36 /02/Add. 02/Rev.01  
Page 192 
 
 
irrigation, a griculture and forestry, and provincial planning and d evelopment depart ments ; and 
community -based organizations, communities and tribal leaders, academic institutions, 
environmental experts, social activists, and private sector actors. Furthermore, the consultation 
process included focus group discussions with women at the co mmunity level to obtain a better 
understanding of their needs relat ing to climate change. 
27. Even though k ey ministries such as water resources, climate change , forestry  and 
agriculture committed to working with departmental programming officers to integrate the project ’s positive results into their operations , it is still unclear how the national government 
intends to mobilize the funds needed to ensure the continuity and maintenance of the proposed project ’s activities. In this respect, it is recommended tha t the project management work s with 
the established authorities to formulate, from the very first years of the proposed project, a 
realistic and sustainable strategy and plan for mobilizing resources for actions integrating EbA, 
green and green -grey infras tructure  beyond the project implementation period . 
28. Country ownership is rated  as High.  
1.7 Efficiency and effectiveness               Scale: Medium  to high  
29. On the basis of  the results of the c ost–benefit analysis carried out in the context of the 
proposed project, the package of interventions envisaged is cost -beneficial. The total project 
investment will generate a net present value of US D 30 million with a benefit- to-cost ratio of 1:1 
and an e conomic inte rnal rate of return of approximately 13.6  per cent.  
30. The project has no government co -finance, as a result of the diversion of funds to relief 
efforts after the major floods of 2022, but  the AE is able to make a small contribution and to 
secure an  addition al USD  10 million in co -financing  from Coca Cola and USAID , bringing the GCF  
to co-financing ratio to 1:0.18.  
31. To inform the design of EbA and green and green -grey  infrastructure interventions, the 
proposed project includes  best practices from similar projects includ ing an optimized set of 
adaptation approaches that are described as “ EbA and green infrastructure ” in the funding 
proposal, but which are not all narrow ly “green”, and  can be considered  to include soft 
engineered or hybrid “green -grey ” measures, such as flood protection embankments, dispersal 
embankments, gabion bunds, retention areas and recharge basin s.  
32. In addition to the information gathered during th e stakeholder consultation process, the 
results of the cost –benefit analysis and the case studies ( notably from China1 and Afghanistan2) 
greatly guided the design of the project ’s interventions. Indeed, the approach used in the case of 
the China project served as a model for most of the green infrastructure and EbA interventions 
considered by the proposed project.  
33. According to  the Working Group II contribution to the I ntergovernmental Panel on 
Climate Change  (IPCC) Sixth Assessment Report, the “understanding of EbA has improved 
considerably but there is medium agreement regarding its benefits (Doswald et al., 2014; Nalau et al., 2018a) ”
3 and “limited evidence and low agreement on its economic efficiency and long -

term effectiveness .” This issue was  raised during the question -and-answer  session with the AE. 
In response to the question  the AE  provided a list of peer -reviewed literature and reports to 
support the efficacy results of the proposed interventions. However, some of them did not 
directly address the issues raised.  
34. Given that the interventions envisaged will be screened  again , as indicated in the project 
document, it would be important to take into account further scientific references confirming 
their effectiveness when making the final ch oice.  In particular, published literature on the 
groundwater resources of the Indus b asin and the potential for aquifer recharge using monsoon 
floodwaters should be consulted.4 
35. Furthermore, establishing evidence of the effectiveness of the EbA and green 
infrastructure interventions is a fundamental element of the proposed project. However, the 
implementation plan does not indicate when the monitoring  and evaluation plan will be 
developed. According to t he answers provided to the questions submitted by the independent 
Technical Advisory Panel (iTA P) and the statements made by the AE  during the above -
mentioned question -and-answer session , this aspect will be taken into account from the earliest 
stages of the proposed project. With this in mind, it is strongly recommended that the AE 
present a clear protocol for this complex task of assessing the effectiveness of the proposed interventions.  This protocol should be validated by peers to ensure its scientific robustness and 
consequently guarantee the validity of the evaluation process.  
36. Efficiency and effectiveness of the proposed project is rated  as Medium  to High. 
II. Overall remarks from the ind ependent Technical Advisory Panel  
37. Based on the above explanations, iTAP  endorses the funding proposal “ Recharge 
Pakistan: Building Pakistan’s resilience to climate change through Ecosystem -based Adaptation 
(EbA) and Green Infrastructure for integrated floo d risk management”.  
38. The iTAP recommends the following improvements to the funding proposal:  
(a) The formulation, from the very first years of the proposed project, of a realistic and 
sustainable strategy and plan for mobilizing resources for actions integrating EbA, green and green -grey infrastructures ;  
(b) The formulation, at the early stage of the project implementation, of a clear protocol for assessing the efficiency a nd effectiveness of EbA and green and green -grey 
infrastructure interventions;  
(c) Making  an additional effort to better take into account scientific and technical references 
in the design of the EbA, green and green -grey infrastructure interventions ; and  
(d) Due to the risk of disease propagation associated with the establishment of retention 
ponds (resulting from the probability of standing water), it is important to carefully 
reassess these risks and to ensure that appropriate mitigation measures are put in place.  
39. Considering that the proposal has  largely satisfied all the GCF’s investment criteria, the 
above recommendations are left to the discretion of the AE .

Overall remarks from the independent Technical Advisory Panel:  
 
We would like to express our appreciation to the iTAP for their overall remarks on this 
project  and for the recommendations provided.  
(a)During implement ation  under Sub-Activity 2.1.1.4 , the Government of
Pakistan will develop a proposed plan for futu re EbA and grey -green
infrastructure projects in additional sites. This will  include a proposed
resource mobilization plan . The Government of Pakistan and its donor
partners, such as USAID , have expressed an interest in supporting the
replication of these interventions in other sites beyond the Recharge Pakistan
project.
(b)Under Sub-Activity 2.1.1.2, the project will establish protocols  for assessing
the efficiency and effectiveness of EbA and green and green -grey
i
nfrastructure interventions on ecosystems and their flood attenuation and
water resource -related services . We will also subject the protocols to peer
review (ideally through a third -party scientific journal), to ensure their
credibility and make them more widely available to the adaptation
community , including the GCF .
(c)Additional efforts will be undertaken to tak e into account  other relevant
scientific and technical references in the design of the EbA, and green -grey
i
nfrastructure interventions . Under the project, a PhD level freshwater
scientist will be  responsible for ensuring that additional scientific references
are consulted .
(
d)The project ’s ESMF  has been updated to include the risk of waterborn e
d
iseases associated with the establishment of retention ponds . To mitigat e
t
hese risks  all project outputs, meaning all activities under a given output, in
each landscape will be subject to the project’s ESS screening , which includes a
category on health and safety , to identify possible risks and create any
necessary mitigation measures or plans.
